[trialAccount t18361128-186]
186. SUSAN OLDER was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of October, 2 knives, value 1 s., 2 forks, value 8 d.; 1 pair snuffers, value 1 s. 6 d.; 4 spoons, value 10 d.; 1 shaving-box, value 6 d.; 1 brush, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 ounce of sewing silk, value 2 s. 6 d.; 3 pieces of tape, value 3 d.; 4 reels of cotton, value 4 d. 1 ounce of pins, value 3 d.; 25 needles, value 3 d.;
1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 1 pocket, value 6 d.; the goods of James Lindsey, her master.
REBECCA LINDSEY . I am the wife of James Lindsey, who keeps a general shop at Lewisham. The prisoner was in our service for eight months—she used to dress in the top room—I had occasion to go up there, and found an article which ought not to be there—I called her up, and pointed out a large tooth comb, a small tooth comb, and hair brush—they are things which we sell—I asked her whose they were—she said they were her own, and she had bought them at London—I pointed out our shop mark on the brush—I sent for Mr. Barham the constable, and all the articles stated were found in a box which I had lent her—she had the key, but it was not locked.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was the mark on the brush a private mark of your own? A. Yes—I made it myself—our shop had been repaired, and there were workman about—a number of things had been taken up to the prisoner's room in a basket—I found the shaving box, knives, and snuffers, at the bottom of her box—the pins and needles were in her dress, and the combs inside the dress—the large comb was on the table under the looking-glass, and the brush and small tooth comb in the dressing-drawer of the table—I could have seen them at any time if I had looked into the drawer.
COURT. Q. Was it necessary to move the things out of the box to see them? A. Yes—they were all at the bottom, except the pins and needles, which were in the dress, and I think the pockets fell out of the dress—the combs and brushes were very nearly new—she had used them.
GEORGE BARHAM . I am a constable of Lewisham. I produce the articles stated in the indictment—the prisoner slept in the kitchen, but dressed in the room up stairs—I found all the things in the box except the stockings which Mrs. Lindsey found in the bed, and the brush and combs—the stocking are old and very much darned.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the prisoner present when you found them? A. Yes—as I was going along with her, she said the shop had been under great alterations, and there was great confusion in moving the things out, and a quantity of things had been taken up to the room; that her mistress sent her up for a basket, and these things being in it, in her hurry to take
the basket down, she shot the things out of the basket into the box—there was only one article of wearing apparel covering the aritcles, and the box was not locked—on moving the first article, these things could be seen—I believe the shop was then finished.
REBECCA LINDSEY re-examined. The things had been in a clothesbasket, but when I sent them up they were all in parcels—there were a dozen knives and forks tied in two papers—I have the paper here; and when I came to have the things down again, I said to her, "They are sadly out of condition," I missed two knives and two forks, and she said, "I cannot think what could possess me to take the things"—those were the words she used—she admitted taking the whole of the things.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you tell that of the Magistrate? A. I do not think I did—she did not say so till the night I found them—when I missed them I asked her if she knew any thing about them, and she said, "No"—I did not tell the Magistrate she acknowledged taking them—I was too much frightened—I did not know I had need to say it—I did not know that the question was put to me—my next door neighbour was present when the prisoner acknowledged taking them—I have never had any quarrel with the prisoner—when I had occasion to complain to her I did so.
LUCY TRUSCOTT . I went into Mrs. Lindsey's house to order some things—Mrs. Lindsey said she had found out that her servant had robbed her—the prisoner was in the parlour, cleaning it, and could hear what passed—Mrs. Lindsey asked me to go up stairs to search her box—she called to the prisoner, and said, "We are going up, Susan, to search your box"—she said, "You are quite at liberty to do that"—we went up, and she said, "Now, Susan, let me look at your box"—she first produced a box with some rags in it—her mistress said, "Have not you another box, which I lent you?"—she produced it, and her mistress said, "Now, open that"—she opened it, and a pocket fell out—she said, "Now, take out the rest of the things"—she took out her gown, and the needles and pins fell out, and at the bottom were all the other things—the knives were loose, not in paper—they did not appear to have been used—we locked the door and came down—the officer was sent for; and after he had taken out all the things, her mistress said, "I suspect the comb you have in your head is the one I have lost"—she took off her cap, pulled it out of her hair, and said, "Yes it is"—when we were up stairs, and had found all the things I said, "Susan, you have done a very bad thing"—she wrung her hands and said she did not know what possessed her to do what she had done.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you find any stockings in the box? A. No.
REBECCA LINDSEY re-examined. Q. Had the prisoner any hair-brush of her own? A. I never saw one—the stockings had not been put away to be washed—I do not know how long she had had them—I am certain they have been taken out of my drawer, and the pocket also, and she has picked the letters out of my handkerchief—here is the L on it now, but part of the the R is taken out—she never bought a brush of me.
Cross-examined. Q. Might not the mark be rubbed out in the washing? A. No—the things were left in the box till the officer came.
JURY. Q. What wages had she? A. Eighteen-pence a week with board and lodging—the knives were safely packed up when I sent them up.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18361128-187]
187. WILLIAM POOLE and THOMAS HAWKES were indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of November, 1 sack, value 2 s., and 20lbs. weight of hay, value 1 s.; the goods of Alfred Courthope, the master of the said William Poole.
ALFRED COURTHOPE . I am a stage-coach proprietor, and live at the Globe in Deptford Lower-road. Poole was my horse-keeper—he was with me about three months the last time, and was previously in my service—in consequence of suspicion I and Bradshaw watched, on the 22nd of November, in an omnibus close to the stable door—Poole came and began his work, and in about half an hour Hawkes came down—in two or three minutes they came up the yard—Poole said to him, "This is a pretty time to come in the morning—it is time I had my dram long ago"—they went out, and returned in about ten minutes, and went down to the stable—Poole took a sack out of loft, put some hay into it, and threw it down in the yard—Hawkes put it on his back and came out of the yard—we watched him into his brother's shop, and caught shooting the sack down—it contained a mixture of hay and clover—I have every reason to believe it was part of mine—I had seen the sack empty in the stable the night before—the contents were worth a shilling.
Cross-examined. MR. PAYNE. Q. Is this the sort of stuff which would come from the sweeping of the loft? A. No—it had been drawn for trusses—Hawkes is the brother of my farrier—the sack has the name of Pavit on it.
JOHN BRADSHAW ( police-constable R 126.) I was with the prosecutor, and saw Poole come in about five minutes to six o'clock—shortly after, Hawkes came on the premises—they both went away for about five minutes, and then returned—Poole went up into the loft with a sack, put the fodder in, and brought it down to the yard—Hawkes took it up—we followed him to his brother's premises, where he emptied it down.
Cross-examined. Q. Is not his brother's a farrier's shop? A. Yes—there was a horse there ready to eat it up—it is not such stuff as would be swept up from the loft—it is a mixture of rye grass and clover.
(The prisoner Hawkes received an excellent character.)
POOLE— GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Six Months.
HAWKES— GUILTY. Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy. Confined Two Days.

188. MARY M'CARTHY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of November, 28lbs. of cheese, value 15 s., the goods of Frederick Pearce.
FREDERICK PEARCE . I am a cheesemonger, and live in High-street, Deptford. WILLIAM COATES . ( police-constable R 129.) The prisoner was given into
my custody by Mr. Pearce—she told me she took the cheese by mistake for a piece of bacon which she had bought—I found the bacon in her basket, and 9 s. 7 d. on her, and two 41lb. loaves.
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Months.

189. ROBERT HAMMOND was indicted for stealing on the 16th of November, 1 saw, value 2 s.; the goods of Joseph Cook: and 1 plane, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Thomas Hudson: and 1 jacket, value 10 s., the goods of Thomas William Harwood.
THOMAS WILLIAM HARWOOD . I am a carpenter, and live at Royal-hill. Greenwich. This jacket is mine—on Wednesday the 16th of November I left it in Mr. Joseph Cook's workshop—I missed it that evening, about six o'clock—this saw belongs to Mr. Cook—it was on the same premises—I went to the shop on Friday morning, and the property was missed.
FRANCIS WASLEY . I am apprentice to Mr. Carter, a pawnbroker, at Greenwich. I produce a plane and saw, which I took in pledge on the 16th of November—I believe the prisoner to be the person who pawned them—I am not quite certain—I gave the person a duplicate.
WILLIAM CANT ( police-constable R 168.) On the morning of the 21st of November I went to Mr. Greenlet, a pawnbroker at Deptford, and the prisoner was given into my custody, with a jacket—Mr. Greenlet said that he was offering it in pawn, and that it was stolen from Greenwich—I asked the prisoner where he stole it—he said he had not stolen it, but bought it of a man with a bag over his shoulder, at a public-house; and if I would go to the public-house with him, the landlady saw him buy it, and knew all about it—I took him there—the landlady said she had not seen him at the house, and knew nothing of it.
Prisoner. The lady said she was afraid to say I had been there, as she did not like to come here.
ELIZA WERRY . I am servant to Mr. Cook, in Royal Circus-street. On the 16th of November I saw the prisoner standing at the shop door—he went in, and came out with a lap full of shavings—he had nothing when he went in—he had no business there.
Prisoner. Q. Where were you standing at the time? A. At the staircase window—he walked towards the bench, where the jacket lay, and then walked back again—I did not look to see if he had taken any thing.
(The prisoner pleaded poverty.)
GUILTY of stealing the jacket only. Aged 52.
Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18361128-190]
190. JANE TYER and JANE ALEXANDER were indicted for burglsriously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of our Lord the King, about the hour of ten in the night of the 8th of November, with intent to steal, and stealing therein. 3 table-cloths, value 30 s.; 1 pillow-case, value 6 d.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 5 s.; 3 handkerchiefs, value 18 d.; 1 printed book, value 2 s.; 1 box, value 1 d.; 3 studs, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 ring, value 4 s.; and 84 moon-stones, value 5 s.; the goods of Berney Varlo.
BERNEY VARLO . I am a lieutenant in the Royal Marines. At the time in question I was quartered at Woolwich, in Frances-street, in houses rented by Government, and occupied by officers of the Marines—the Government pay the rent and taxes. On Tuesday evening, the 8th of November, I left my quarters about half-past six o'clock—I was absent nearly
six hours—on my return I missed my key—every thing was replaced in my quarters by the sergeant of the Guard before I returned—I never authorized the prisoners to take any of my property.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are you a single man? A. I am—I have known Alexander about a month, and Tyer about three years—Tyer has continually called at my quarters, but Alexander only once—they had been there together about five days before the 8th of November—they were in the habit of visiting me together—Alexander has been twice—Tyer had been in the habit of visiting me before my return—I had recently returned from abroad—when they came together one staid half an hour, and the other about two hours, and drank—Tyer was in the habit of drinking when she came—we were on terms of very intimate familiarity—sometimes she has staid four or five hours, and sometimes more or less—I generally treated her with liquor when she came—she introduced Alexander to me.
Q. Now, I ask you, were not these persons on terms of such familiarity with you that they might be induced to think they had some title to make free with your property? A. Certainly not in my absence—Tyer has been in the habit of sitting with me in my quarters for three or four hours together drinking—they generally took wine, and occasionally spirits and water—I first got acquainted with Tyer in Woolwich—I think she was in service at that time—she has visited me a great many times.
CHARLES HAYES . I am a sergeant in the Royal Marines. On the 8th of November I went to the prosecutor's quarters about ten o'clock and found the prisoner Tyer lying on the top of the area, against the front door, on her back, very drunk indeed—one part of her body was through the gate, partly in the area of the next house, and partly out, with three table-cloths and a pair of trowsers lying within a foot of her, and some might be two feet from her—the front door and private door of the prosecutor's house were both wide open—Tyer was so drunk that she had no senses—I took a light to see whether she was dead or alive—she was frothing at the mouth just as if she was mad—I thought at first she was dead—I looked at her for some time, and then went into the prosecutor's quarters, and on the table there was a small basket with several articles in it—there was a pillow-case marked with his name on it—I left it there, locked the door, and took the key with me, and put a sentry there—when the prosecutor returned I gave him the key and his property—I went with him to his quarters about a quarter before eleven o'clock, and found the door as I had left it—I had Tyer taken to the guard room—Alexander had come crying to me about ten o'clock, saying, that Tyer had been in Lieutenant Varlo's quarters, and taken out some property, and she went back with me to the prosecutor's quarters—she had been drinking, as she smelt strong of liquor.
Cross-examined. Q. Tyer was in such a state it was impossible she could know what she was doing? A. It was utterly impossible.
EDWARD LAPPAGE . I am a private in the Royal Marines, in the prosecutor's service. On Tuesday evening the 8th of November, the prisoners came to master's quarters about six o'clock, and told me they had met my master out in the street, and he had told them I was to give them some liquor—I told them I could not do so unless master was in, and if they wanted it they must come when he was at home—I then locked the door and they went away—I left the key in the room door—the prisoners were quite sober then—the outer door has a spring lock—I did not bolt
that till I left for the night—I went into the kitchen when I locked his room door—I left the street door open for my master—when I left his room the property stood in the clothes-box—I saw a pair of trowsers, a pillow-case, and other things in a clothes-basket in the room, and a prayer. book on the desk, with others—I had seen it there about two hours before the prisoners came.
Cross-examined. Q. When your master's door was opened you cannot tell? A. No—I had never seen the prisoners at the house before—I have lived with my master about two months—I told the Magistrate that they told me they came for liquor—I am sure they read over to me about the liquor in my deposition.
JOSEPH ALLCHIN . I am a watchman. On Tuesday night, the 8th of November, I had information given me, and went to the guard-room, with the sergeant—I saw Tyer lying in the guard-room—I searched her, and found nothing on her—I took her to the watch-house and left her—I found this pillow-case, table-cloth, and trowsers in the prosecutor's room—next morning I asked Tyer in what manner she entered Mr. Varlo's quarters—she said, while they were in search after the key she found it on the ledge of the door, and unlocked the door herself—Alexander was not present—I found a shawl in the prosecutor's room, which Tyer claimed
CHARLES STEWART . I am superintendent of the parochial✗ police at Woolwich. On the 9th of November I took Alexander into custody at her father's—I told her I apprehended her from information I had received, as being in company with Jane Tyer, concerned in the robbery at the quarters of Lieutenant Varlo—she said she was present previous to the robbery in company with Tyer, but she did not commit it—I searched her, and found a prayer-book, a small box containing moon-stones, a ring, and three studs—I had previously searched Tyer at the watch-house, and three pocket handkerchiefs dropped from under her gown—I have these here.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you not forgotten something which Alexander said to you? A. I have not forgotten it—she told me before the things were taken she had been with Tyer to Mr. Varlo's quarters, that Mr. Varlo's servant locked the room door, and then she went away, and was to Woolwich Common with another person; that when she returned, she found Tyer lying on the ground, and the linen lying about, and that she picked the things I found off the steps.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin
[trialAccount t18361128-191]
191. MARGARET FORD was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of Novvember, 1 half-sovereign, 2 sixpences, and 1 purse, value 1 d.; the goods and monies of James Owens from his person.
JAMES OWENS . I am a pensioner. On Sunday I went to the Eight-Bells public-house at Greenwich—I was not at all fresh—I had just come from chapel—I had about 12 s. 6 d. in a purse in my right-side trowsers pocket, there was a half-sovereign among it—I had been drinking there from about two o'clock in the afternoon, till six o'clock—I was not drunk—I did not see the prisoner till she introduced herself—she was a stranger—when I asked her to drink, she saw I had a purse, and some money—she shoved me down from the seat, and fell upon me, and put her hand into my pocket, and took my purse and money—I saw her do it—I called to her to give it me, she
would not—I fetched policeman—he found the purse, but I was not present—I know it is mine.
JOSEPH BISCHLAYER . Between five and six o'clock in the afternoon, I went into Eight Bells—I saw the prosecutor and the prisoner drinking together; the prisoner called for a pot of beer or half and half, and the prosecutor would not pay for it, the landlord took it back, and then in a few minutes she shoved him off the bench, she fell on him, and he called out, "Are you going to rob me"?—she got up, and had the purse in her hand, and shook out some money, but what I could not see—she took one piece and put it into her mouth, and then showed me two sixpences—I advised her to give it him back again, and then she put the two sixpences, behind her, and threw the purse away—she said, "Let the old b----r go, we will spend the money together.
EVAN EVANS . I was at the public-house, and saw the prisoner pulling the money out of the purse into her hand—she threw the purse on the ground—I saw her jump on the prosecutor—Biscklayer's evidence is true.
JOHN REESE ( police-constable R 79.) I was called—I found the purse behind her on the table—I found two sixpences on the prisoner.
JAMES OWENS . This purse is mine.
Prisoner's Defence. He came to the stall I was sitting, and said, "It is very cold"—I said, "Yes," another woman said, "You might give us a pint of beer?"—he said he did not mind giving me a glass of grog, and then he went in and had some porter, and then some more, and then he called for some more, and would not pay for it"—I was in liquor, and I own I snatched the purse from his hand, and said he should pay for it.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.

192. ELIZABETH LANE was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of November, 1 bag, value 2 d. 4 half-crowns, and 15 shillings; the goods and monies of Frederick Clossie.
MARTHA CLOSSIE . My husband's name is Frederick, he is a Greenwich pensioner, and a teacher of music. On the 8th of November, Mrs. Cooper brought the prisoner to me to get her a lodging, as she had just come from London, out of place—she lodged and boarded with me till the 14th—she was there about six days—these four half-crowns and fifteen shillings were in a box by the side of the dire-place, in a little brown holland bag with twine round it—she said she was going a little way, and then she should come back, but she did not—I saw the money safe at half past eleven o'clock, and missed it about two o'clock—I have not got any of my money since.
FREDERICK CLOSSIE . I was sitting a the table, and heard the box move, and then the prisoner went away—I told my wife I heard, and the money was gone—the prisoner left at two o'clock on Monday the 14th ( This witness was blind.).
MARY DOYLE . The prisoner came to our house to see my mother on Monday—she said when she was there before that she had nothing to give, but then she had a half-crown, and sent Mrs. Doyle for some liquor.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Justice Vaughan.

193. FREDERICK ROSSER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of November, at St. Nicholas, Deptford, 4 watches, value 7 l.; 4 seals, value 20 s.; 4 keys, 6 d.; 1 chain value 30 s.; and three watchribbons, value 4 d.; the goods of Charles May, in his dwelling-house.
CHARLES MAY . I am a watch-maker, live in Union-street, in the parish of St. Nicholas, Deptford. I was called down stairs on Wednesday morning, the 16th of November, and in consequence of what my sister said I ran out, and heard the cry of "Stop thief"—I ran through part of High-street into Loving Edward's-lane—I saw a man running, and saw him throw some watches into a ditch on the left-hand side—I ran to the spot, and waited there till three of the watches were taken out—the person who threw them there ran on pursued—by several persons—the watches had been intrusted to me to be repaired—I should think they are worth 7 l.—they had hung against the wall in my shop—I had seen them about a quarter of as hour before—I had not authorized any body to take them.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you at any time state that the person you saw running had a white apron on? A. Yes—the prisoner was in custody three or four minutes after I saw him running—I saw him in custody some time before the watches were got of the ditch—he had a white apron on when taken—Loving Edward's-lane is 150 or 200 yards from my house—he had his back to me me when threw the watches away.
JOHN LILL . I am a letter-carrier in the Post-office. On the morning in question I heard a cry of "Stop thief, and saw the prisoner turn the corner of Union-street, and run down Edwards-lane—I ran after him, and when a few paces from him be threw something into the ditch—I stopped there, and saw three watches taken out—I turned round, and met him fall in the face as he ran past me—he had an apron on—I never lost sight of him till he was taken.
Cross-examined. Q. Were there other people running? A. Yes, several, but I was next to the prisoner—there might be a few before me—I was about three yards from him—there was nobody between me and him—a person crossed us at the time he threw the watches away—I will not swear the person had not a white apron on—the prisoner continued running, and was stopped—the person who crossed us was not stopped—I was passing along—Mr. May was beind me.
JAMES CONNER . I am a constable. On the day in question I was walking through High-street, and saw the prisoner running in Union-street, towards me—I saw Mr. May run out of his house, without his hat—I followed the prisoner directly—he ran into Edward's-lane, and on the left hand threw something from the bib of his apron with his left hand into the ditch—I stopped about a second to look at the ditch, but could see nothing—I followed him immediately and at the end of lane, by the houses, a witness ran a few yards a-head of me and stopped him—I crossed the road directly, and took him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see a man cross him with an apron on? A. No—if there had been. I should think I must have seen him—to the best of my recollection nobody was a-head of me but Turner—the prisoner had an apron on when I took him.
JOSEPH WHITTAL . I am a baker at Deptford. I examined the ditch and found three or four watches—I am not certain which—I gave them to Mr. May—I saw the prisoner running as fast as he could on the same side of the way as the ditch.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you an apron on? A. No—there were several persons running after him—the prisoner had a white apron on—I
did not see any body cross the prisoner at the time the things were thrown into the ditch.
JOHN TAYLOR . I am a constable. I was near Mr. May's house about ten minutes before the robbery, and saw the prisoner standing on the step of Mr. May's door—the street door was open at the time—there is a passage, and a door in the middle of the passage into the shop—as I returned, in about ten minutes, I just turned round the corner of Union-street, and heard a call of "Stop him"—I turned round, and saw prisoner running across High-street, and Mr. May after him—I ran and followed—I saw something go from prisoner's left hand into the ditch—I ran up the lane, and saw Turner stop him—he had a black frock coat and a white apron on.
WILLIAM TURNER . I ran after the prisoner up Loving Edward's-lane, and stopped him—he said, "Let me go, I have only broken a window"—and then the policeman came up—I did not see him with any thing—he ran alongside of the ditch—I saw his hand go out by the side of the ditch, but did not see him throw any thing into the ditch.
Cross-examined. Q. How near were you to him when saw his hand go? A. Within a few yards—I saw him throw something away—I cannot say what it was.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
JURY. to MR. MAY. Q. Do you value the watches at 7 l., at the wholesale or retail price? That is the value they would readily fetch—I do not deal in second-hand watches—they might not fetch that at an auction—7 l. is her fair value.
GUILTY. of stealing under 5 l. —Aged 20. Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Justice Vaughan.

194. JAMES ELLIOTT was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Brittain, on the 7th of November, at Greenwich, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 watch, value 1 l. 5 s.; 1 watch-key, value 1 d.; 1 shirt-front, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 coat, value 1 l. 10 s., 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l.; 1 necklace, value 4 s.; 1 watch-guard, value 10 s.; 1 ring, value 2 s.; 1 shawl, value 40 s.; 1 shirt value 2 s., and 3 waistcoats, value 15 s.; his goods; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ROSINA BRITTAIN . I am the wife of William Brittain, a bricklayer, at Maidstone-hill, in the parish of Greenwich. On the 7th of November I left the house about five minutes before twelve o'clock, and left the door on the spring-lock—the windows were safe—I left a watch-ribbon and key in the house, and the other things stated in the indictment—I am sure they were all safe before I left—I missed them when I returned—we have moved since.
FRANCES BRITTAIN . I am the prosecutrix's mother-in-law. I went to the house on Maidstone-hill on the 9th October—I received the key from my daughter-in-law's little girl, who I met on London-bridge—I opened the door with it, and about ten minutes after I got in I perceived there was something not right, and missed the metal watch, and a suit of my son's clothes—I saw my son next morning, and informed the police as soon as I got to town.
WILLIAM GEORGE OSBORNE . I am a policeman. In consequence of information from the prosecutrix I went in search of the prisoner—I apprehended him on the 11th of November in Greenwich road, going towards
Deptford—I searched him, and found a tobacco-box in the lining of his hat, and the duplicate of a watch pawned at Barham's in the name of "Thomas Jones"—I went there and found the watch.
JAMES PARRY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner from the station-house after he had been there, and found a shirt front on him, which he was wearing—he said it was his own.
WILLIAM HUNT . I am shopman to Mr. Barham, a pawnbroker is Princes-street, Soho. I received a watch in pawn from the prisoner, is the name of Thomas Jones, for 14 s.—I am sure he is the man—this is the duplicate I gave him.
Prisoner. I did not pawn it; it was a young man named Jones Witness. I am quite sure of him—I saw him at Green with a few days after.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the ticket of a young man at Rotherhithe, and a young man named Jones, man named Jones, a carpenter, got it out, and pawned it in another place—two young men saw me buy it.
JAMES WILD . I am policeman. I produce a copy of the record of the conviction of the prisoner ( read)—I was present at his trial, and as certain he is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Life.

195. JOHN DAY was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Charles, Stokes, on the 2nd of September, at Greenwich, with intent to steal, and steling therein, 1 boot, value 4 s.; and 1 shoe, value 18 d.; his goods.
CHARLES STOKES . I am a shoemaker, and live in Stockport-street Greenwich. About half-past twelve o'clock on the 2nd of September, I heard my window smash, and heard a cry of "Stop thief"—I ran down stairs, and saw one of my windows broken, and missed a boot and shoe—I ran into the street, and saw the prisoner in the hands of an officer—the window was quite sound in the morning—the boot and shoes were placed as near to the broken pane as possible.
JOHN REESE . ( police-constable R 79.) I heard the smash of a window in Stockport-street, about twelve o'clock, and heard a cry of "Stop thief"—I immediately went across the road—there was a funeral going along which prevented my going as fast as I should have done—I saw the prisoner close against the window, and running away from it, and he was brought back by Lillywhite, a constable—he dropped a shoe and boot, which I picked up—his right hand was all over blood—I asked him how he came to do it, and he said he did it through want.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I had had no bed for nine nights, and no victuals to eat—I did it entirely from want.
GUILTY. Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant

MARIA CANHAM . I am the wife of Thomas Canham, shipwright, of Coleman-street, Woolwich. I keep haberdasher's shop—I had a shawed like this on the 15th of November—I saw it safe between ten and eleven
o'clock hanging in the window—I heard a footstep in my shop, and saw the prisoner M'Ghee going from the door, and missed a shawl—a few minutes before I had seen him and another boy at the window—I directly ran after M'Ghee and stopped him—I accused him of having been in the shop—he denied it, and said a boy had run round the corner, or down the street—I detained him, and sent for the policeman—while I was detaining him a little girl and her mother brought me the shawl—it is my husband's
MARY ANN GOODCHILD . I live a few doors from Mrs. Canham. I found this shawl in my father's garden, near the gate, about eleven o'clock in the morning, on the 15th of November.
WILLIAM THOMAS CHILLENDEN . I am a Woolwich police-constable. I apprehended Hawkesworth in consequence of information—I did not say a word to him, his confession was purely of his own free will—he stated that M'Ghee and him had gone to the Dock-yard; on returning through Coleman-street they staid some time about the shop, and then M'Ghee brought him the shawl, which he gave to him and told him to cut, and he ran; but being much frightened, he went to the rear of the house, and threw it over the garden—M'Ghee was given into my custody, and he then carried an iron trivet, which he said belonged to him.

197. JAMES DODDERIDGE was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of November, 1 crown, 1 half-crown, and 1 shilling; the monies of Robert Kersey.
ROBERT KERSEY , Jun. The prisoner has been in my father's employ since last Whit Monday. I marked a crown, a half-crown, and one shilling—I placed them in the till with other silver, making 5 l. together about six o'clock in the evening of the 24th of November—and about half-past o'clock the policeman searched the prisoner in my presence—no money was found on him, but a crown and a half-crown piece were found under the chair where he was sitting, and a shilling behind the door—it was all marked money.
WILLIAM KERSEY . I am the witness's brother I was set to detect this youth—about half-past six o'clock I saw him reach over the counter, unlock the till, and draw it out—he took out some money—I could not see what it was, but it was large coin—I waited till my brother came out of the yard, and then I went for the policeman—when I came back the money was on the ground—the prisoner said he would not do it any more.
GEORGE SOLE ( police-constable R 174.) I went and saw the the prisoner sitting in the chair—Mr. Kersey said he had dropped something—I looked under the chair, and found the crown-piece and the half-crown—I then found the shilling behind the door, near shutter that stood there—I found a sixpence, and some copper on the prisoner—in going along th
prisoner met his brother, and he asked what he had done, I said, "Robbed his master of 8 s. 6 l."—he said, "No, it was only 7 s. 6 d."
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 15.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.

[trialAccount t18361128-201]
201. NATHANIEL WOULD was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of November, 40lbs. weight of leaden-pipe, value 6 s. 6 s., the goods of George Oliver and fixed to a certain building; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN REDPATH . I am gardener to Mr. George Oliver, at Blackheath-hill. The prisoner was employed there about two years—I missed about 50 yards of leaden-pipe from my master's garden—it was fixed with staples to a wall—this pipe is similar to it—there was no particular mark on it—I fitted it in the presence of the officer.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You will swear to it? A. No—I saw a portion of it on Saturday the 11th, against the wall, it was
missed on Wednesday the 16th of November; it appeared to correspond—some portion of it might have been gone a month ago—I never missed any before—it was formerly of use, but not for the last two years—the prisoner was a confidential, well-conducted man—immediately I discovered it I went to him, and we both went to search for foot-marks—after that some gentlemen offered 20 l. to some person who would come forward and convict any person who had stolen it.
COURT, Q. Did the prisoner go with you to search? A. Yes, these were no foot-marks.
JURY. Q. Did this fit the place? A. Not exactly, there is some wanting.
THOMAS BAYLEY . I am a plumber. On the 11th of November, about 5 o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to my shop with some pipe—he asked if I would buy it—I asked him if it was his own—he said yes, and he wanted to sell it, as his wife given herself up to drink lately, and had made away with some of it, he thought he had better turn the rest into money—he said he had had it by him some time, and that he had been at work for a former master who gave him this lead instead of wages, as he was thinking of having water laid on at a future time—he told me where he lived, and that he worked for Mr. Oliver, on Blackhealth-hill—on the Wednesday following I heard Mr. Oliver had lost some lead, and as it was similar to this I gave information.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear any thing of the reward? A. I did, I read the bill—that was how I learned that Mr. Oliver had lost lead, the reward made no impression on me—it is twelve months yesterday since I was here—I know the Hospital at Greenwich had some lead stolen from it—Mr. Edward Bayley is my uncle—I was placed in the dock, but discharged without the least stain on my character—I can be trusted now for a thousand pounds—I have not the least doubt that I shall get the reward if the man is convicted, but not if he is acquitted—I believe my brother James was in the shop when the prisoner came in, but I cannot be certain—we have not conversed about the evidence we were to give—we came here in company from Deptford—we never uttered a word about it on the road—there might a word drop now and then, but we had no long conversation—the shop is very large—I have not asked my brother whether he was there—I did not see the prisoner in my shop that day afterwards—I remained in the shop till half-past six o'clock—I believe I told the Magistrate that he said his wife had taken to liquor—I gave information on the Wednesday evening following—this was not reported currently through the village—I have known the prisoner I suppose ten years, or else I should not have bought the lead of him—I have bought lead once of him before—not more to my knowledge.
JAMES BAYLEY . I am brother to Thomas Bayley. I was in the shop on the 11th of November, about half-past 8 o'clock—I bought some leadpipe of the prisoner—it is here—I asked him nothing it—I gave him 3 s. 3 d. for it—it weighed 33lbs.
Cross-examined. Q. Of course you would not have bought it and asked him no questions unless you had know him well? A. I have known him about two years, but was not acquainted with him—I have seen him sell lead in the shop before—my brother assists me in the shop—I only saw him sell lead once before in the shop—he then sold it to my brother—I was present—I suppose that was two or three months before I bought this of him—when we came from Deptford there were two policeman
and my uncle, with a great quantity of men—brother never mentioned to me that the prisoner had sold him Mr. Oliver's lead, till we were before the Magistrate—I am at home sometimes—I was out the night the man called on my brother—I heard of a reward, but I have nothing to do with that—my brother did not take him—I do not know that my brother expected a reward—he did not tell me so—I have heard him mention there was a reward for the lead-pipe—I believe it was for convicting any body accused of stealing it—I have heard my brother name it as well as other—the Magistrate never refused me my expenses—I never went after a certificate, nor did my brother to my knowledge—we never asked.
Q. How often did the prisoner, sell it you? A. Only once—my brother was not in the shop, but I told him of it.
COURT. Q. Did you ask the man who he worked for? A. I knew who he worked for—I put down his name in the book, and where he lived, and what I gave him for it—I did not ask him where he got in them, because I had seen my brother buy lead of him previously—I did not produce the book before the Magistrate.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When did your brother buy of him? A. To the best of my recollection two or three months before—I believe my brother ther then asked him where he lived—his name was down in the book—I am sure I heard my brother ask him—I was present and heard him—my brother knew where he lived, and who he was.
EDWARD BAYLEY . I am a constable. About eight o'clock on Wednesday evening, the 16th, I received information that Mr. Oliver had lost some lead—I went to his premises and waited till the prisoner came out of the stable—I then took him into custody—I told him it was on suspicion of stealing lead from Mr. Oliver—he told me no, I was wrong—I had Thomas Bayley with me—I said, "No, I cannot be wrong, as this is the young man that purchased it of you"—he then said it was not the lead that was stolen from Mr. Oliver's it was lead of his own—I told him he must go with me to the station-house, and then he said he dug the lead out of the ground—I asked him what ground, and whether the lead was his own or Mr. Oliver's, and after a little hesitation he said it was Mr. Oliver's—I went afterwards, with two policemen, into Mr. Oliver's garden to match the lead, and we found it corresponded exactly with where the lead was taken from—I believe the lead belonged to the place—it appeared as if it had been sawn with a saw.
Cross-examined. Q. Quite fresh? A. No, rather discoloured a little at the ends—I am the uncle of the other two Bayleys—Thomas gave me the information on the Wednesday—he said he saw a bill out about some lead of Mr. Oliver's—I saw one as I was going to Mr. Oliver; s—I did not see it till I went to take the prisoner—it offered a reward of 20 l —I did not come here with my nephews—I have had conversation with my nephews about the lead—it is my brother's business, but he is very ill—my nephews attend to it—they keep a book, which they showed to me, with Oliver's name in it, the 11th—there were two entries to his name—I did not see any name before his, but I believe there were some—there was one by Thomas, and one by John—they had the offered to their pockets before the Magistrate—I do not know that they offered to produce it—there was a dispute about the price, and Thomas said he had made the entry in the book, it was 14 s. 10 d.10 d.—he did produce it.
COURT. Q. Do you know what the dispute was about? A. The prisoner
said he had only 9 s. and some odd pence, and Thomas said he had paid 14 s., 10 d., and the book would prove it.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was there any dispute with James Bayley? A. No, the prisoner said that what he said was right—James said he had paid him 3 s. and some odd pence—I had no knowledge of the robbery till the bills were out.
WILLIAM CRAWLEY ( police-constable R 53.) About half past seven o'clock on the night of the 16th of November I was in the watch-house at Greenwich, where the prisoner was confined—he said, "The lead which I sold to Mr. Bayley was some lead I took from the back of the cottage, situated on the premises of Mr. Oliver"—I went to those premises the following morning, and saw some lead had been taken that appeared to correspond with one of the pieces—I had said nothing to him to induce him to make the confession.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he tell it to you without asking him any questions? A. Yes, quite of his own accord—Bayley, the constable, was present, and Kent the inspector of the R division, and Sizer a policeman, and several more—I had no conversation with Mr. Bayley before that, that I am aware of—I heard of the reward—I cannot say who told me—Bayley never told me, to my recollection—I my have mentioned about the reward to the young Bayleys—I heard of this robbery on the night of the 16th—it was from one of the Bayleys.
COURT. Q. Do you know what officers were examining the premises before that? A. No, I never heard of any officer being on the premises before the 16th.
JAMES PARRY ( police-sergeant R 8.) I went to Mr. Oliver's and compared the lead, and about half past eleven o'clock on the night of the 16th I went to the cage, and asked the prisoner, "Whose garden do you mean the lead was in that you left?"—he said "At Mr. Oliver's, at the back of the cottage, where I got this," and he said the remainder of the lead was in his garden—I went to the prisoner's house, and could not find it.
Cross-examined. Q. You asked him whose garden the lead was in that he left? A. Yes, I believe they were the words—I do not think I told the Magistrate I used the word "buried"—I know the Bayley family well—I know of the reward—I have spoken to them about it two or three times—I was before the Magistrate—Thomas Bayley was there—there was not a word said by him before the Magistrate about his having entered any lead in any book.
MR. PHILLIPS called
JOHN DRAKE FINCH . I am clerk to the Magistrates of Woolwich. I attended the examination of these men, and took the depositions—Thomas Bayley did not say one word about his having entered in the book the account of the lead—there was never a word said about a book, I am quite certain—I believe the uncle was present—there was no dispute about the price of the lead.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18361128-202]
202. JOHN BLAGG was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of November, 2 trusses of hay, value 20 s., the goods of our Lord the King.—and 2nd COUNT, stating it to belong to one of the Principal Officers of His Majesty's Ordnance ; and STEPHEN BURLEY , for felonioulsy receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES WOOTTEN . I am a pensioner, employed by Colonel Jones The barracks, and guard-rooms, and stables at Plumstead Marsh are the property of his Majesty—there was a great quantity of hay there belonging to his Majesty—on Sunday morning, the 27th of November, I went and found the lock of the stable had been hampered—I missed from ten to fifteen trusses of hay from these stables which are near the barracks—I informed Corporal Ashcroft, and we traced the hay on the ground over the fence, by the stable-door down the manor, from twelve to fifty yards from Burleys' house—there are four or five houses, besides Burley's—he is a gardener—his house stands on the manor-way in the first row that leads to Plumstead parish—there is a fence round Burley's premises—there is a ditch between his house and the manor, that is, twelve yards from his house one way, and fifty, perhaps, the other—the track of hay ceased at Burley's about fifty yards from his fence—I came back and saw Blagg—he is a gunner, and frequently on duty at the guard-room—Corporal Ashcroft went into the guard-room—I saw Blagg come out—I said, "This is a pretty business"—then he said. "It was I that did it'—(the corporal was not then present)—I had not said any thing about stealing the hay—he said "I have the key"—he did not show me any key—he said nothing more about the key—I said I must make a report, because the property was all littered about the place, from the door—I reported it to Serjeant-Major Bollin—I had been in this stable two days before—we have got two trusses here—I first saw the hay in Burley's shed in the Marsh—I went there on the Monday with the Sergeant-Major—he asked him if he had any hay there, and then we saw some in the shed, part of which is here—it is part of what we missed from the stable—I bound it up myself, and have no doubt of it—I saw it on Friday—I saw about nine trusses at the prisoner's which I had missed form the stable.
JURY. Q. Were there any house adjoining his? A. Yes, and the track was as near to the other houses as his.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I suppose a good deal of hay is grown in the marsh? A. Yes, in some seasons—Burley's is three hundred yards from the stable, and about fifty yards from the high-road—the hay that is carried from the Marsh would not pass by Burley's house—it is conveyed to the stables for the horses'use—there are no other farm-houses handy, there are some in Plumstead parish—those farmers would not carry hay past his house, because it is private ground—all the Marsh belongs to the King—it is not possible for hay to be carried across the Marsh in the way this went—this is, I suppose, two or three hundred yards from the main way—we found the first two trusses in the shed—I recognised them, because I cut and bound them about two months ago—they are not cut and bound like a hay binder does it—I know my own binding—I think I bound this, but I do not know.
MR. DOANE. Q. Had you missed some trusses from the stable? A. Yes—these corresponded exactly with what we had and what we lost—there is no public way across the manor.
ALEXANDER ASHCROFT . I am a corporal in the Royal Artillery. On Sunday morning I traced some hay from the stable to the plantation, at the head of the manor way—I did not go over the plantation that day
after that—I went to the guard-room, where Blagg was—I told him some hay had been carried away during the night, and said if he knew any thing of it he had better tell it—he gave me a key, and a piece of paper with five names on it besides his won—I kept the Key, and tried it to the padlock of the stable door from which the hay had been taken—the key had one ward cut out—I cannot say whether that would injure the lock—I reported to the Serjeant. Major what had happened—I cannot say whether this hay is used exclusively for the horses in the regiment.
Blagg. Q. Is this hay used for the horses of the regiment? A. It is used by Colonel Jones for his horses.
COURT. Q. Do you know whether it has been distributed by the proper officer to Colonel Jones? A. I do not—it is only used by Colonel Jones.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Whose horses are Colonel Jones'? A. They are his own property, but this hay was in the King's stables.
WILLIAM BOYLIN . I am Serjeant-Major of the Royal Riding-horse Artillery. Colonel Jones is commanding-officer of the Riding-house establishment—he has horses there for the purpose of riding—they are fed with the King's hay—on the 27th of November I spoke to Blagg—Corperal Ashcroft and James Wotten were present—they handed me a slip of paper with six men's names on it, and this key—I was ordered to learn the particulars from the commanding-office, and I asked Blagg when he had taken the hay—that after what Ashcroft had said to him.
ALEXANDER ASHCROFT re-examined. It was about ten o'clock in the morning that I said if he knew any thing he had better tell it—I saw him afterwards with the Serjeant-Major about two o'clock in the afternoon.
WILLIAM BOYLIN re-examined. Q. Had you any conversation with the prisoner Blagg on the subject to which that key or list referred? A. No—I saw him again about eight o'clock the next morning—he led me to a yard in the possession, as I understood, of Burley, and there was some hay—I saw Burley there—he did not deny it—he took me to stable—I had no reason to doubt that it was Burley's yard—he came out after I entered the yard—I asked him where the hay was which he had purchased from Blagg—he said he had purchased no hay—I said the prisoner had confessed bringing some hay there, and also had led me to the place—he said he had not purchased any, but he found three trusses there on Sunday morning—I asked where it was—he pointed to a shed and said it was there—we found there two trusses of hay—I asked him where the third truss was—he said he knew nothing about it—I then asked him if he had a stable—he said, "Yes" he went and opened the door with a latch—he went in, and there was a horse feeding at the rack—I took a portion of the hay out of the rack, and said, "I suppose this is part of the third truss?"—Bombardier Wilson went further into the stable, and found part of a truss—he called to me and said, "Here is part of the truss"—this was all in Burley's presence—he then went into a loft above the stable, and began counting some other trusses—I got upon a kind of hurdle, so that I could see into the loft, and he counted, in the stable and loft, eight trusses, including the broken truss—I then left a sentry in charge of it, while I went and communicated it to the commanding officer—I did not go again—Burely came to the barracks and asked me whether he could see Colonel Jones—I said he was gone to the office with the reports, but I was going there also, and would let him know—I said he was very foolish for trafficking with a soldier in any such way, or something to that effect—he then said he had been troubling him
since last winter, and he would rather pay any thing than it should go before the public, as he had been between twenty-five and twenty-six years a resident in that place, and had not a stain on his character—I told the commanding officer, who told me to give him into custody—I have examined the hay found in his stable and place—it is exactly of the same description as that in the barracks—the trusses are here—Burley has nothing to do with the barracks in any way—he was not in the service that I am aware of—Blagg has to be at the stable, when he is on guard, every third or fourth day—he ought not to have had access to the place, but he had the opportunity.
Cross-examined. MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How long have you been in the regiment? A. About twenty-six or twenty-seven years—Blagg was with me about seven or eight weeks before that—there is a regular allowance of hay for the officers' horses—I do not know whether this was a portion of Colonel Jones's—I cannot say whether this hay was allotted to him as an officer, or in any other capacity.
JURY. Q. Could he order any quantity of hay to be taken out of these stables? A. I dare say he might, but I do not know any thing of it—he had some cows—I do not know whether they were fed with the King's hay—I will undertake to say it is the same sort as that is the stables.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Where was this shed? A. In Burley's yard—any body passing by could have seen it, but not the inside of it—the opening was from the stable—I cannot say whether it could be seen from the road—there was no sort of concealment on the part of Burley—he denied that he had purchased any hay—I have brought one truss from the stable, and one from the shed.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Is the hay in his Majesty's stables until it is delivered out to an officer? Yes—I went with Blagg to Burley's and spoke to him about some hay that Blagg had delivered, and then went into the shed an stable and found the hay.
COURT. to JAMES WOOTTEN. Q. You said you saw some hay at the prisoner's? A. Yes—I have compared the two trusses with the hay in the bulk—they agree—I believe they are both part of the bulk, and part of what I trussed.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. What connexion have you with the government? A. None, except having a pension—I bind hay for the Colonel, and receive wages from him—I make the bands and tie hay up.
JURY. Q. If there was other hay on Colonel Jones's premises you would know? A. I do not go to the stables.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Has he more hay than this? A. I cannot say, the hay may go to the barracks for the horses or cows—the hay was in the King's stables, the day of these trusses is like the hay that is there.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Looking at the hay by band and the doing up do you believe it to be what you made up? A. I have not the least doubt, it is that I made from the King's land, and put into the stable.
MR. DOANE to CORPORAL ASHCROFT. Q. Is this the key you tried to the stable door in which the hay was seen before it was stolen? A. Yes, and this key opened the stable door.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18361128-203]
203. JOHN TAYLOR was indicted for breaking and entering a building within the curtilage of the dwelling-house of James Farqahar on the 26th of November, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 8 bushels of apples, value 2 l.; half a peck of walnuts, value 1 s.; 2 sacks, value 1 s.; and 7 quarts of pea seeds, value 4 s. 8 d.; his goods.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH HILL GREGORY . I am gardener to Mr. James Farquhar of Charlton. On his premises there is a tool-house, where apples and things of that sort are kept—it is within the fence that encloses the ground—you can go to it from the house without going outside the fence—on Saturday the 26th of November, I fastened it myself, about five o'clock in the evening—the apples, walnuts, and baskets were all safe—I went the following morning about ten o'clock, and found some persons had entered by taking a pane of glass out of the casement, that would enable them to force back the lock of the door—it appeared that that had been done—I missed from seven to nine bushels of apples, two sacks, some seed peas, some walnuts, and a gathering basket—these apples are the same kind that were lost—I know this sack by the particular way in which the mouth is torn—I had used it a day or two before—there is the other sack—I know it by being a very large one—I am certain it is the same—the apples correspond with what they left—I have got a sample of them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Are these sacks all alike? A. Two of them I know nothing of, the other two are not similar, they have no marks of my master's name on them—there was some long name on one of them—my master's sacks were in my keeping—I only swear to the one with the tear, that is the only reason—I have never seen sacks torn, in that way.
MR. BODKIN. Q. You had been using this sack a day or two before? A. Yes, and one that lost, which had a wide mouth—I believe this is the same—it was shown to me on the Monday.
JAMES WILD ( police-constable R 141.) I went to the prosecutor's premises, and I saw the way the tool-house had been entered—I observed foot marks, and covered them over—they proceeded towards the place that had been entered, and towards a wood—they were plainly visible, and I covered them over—I afterwards went to the wood in company with the gardener, and found these two sacks of apples—they ware concealed in the wood, and covered over with the branches—there was a gathering-basket close to the sacks—I remained there from half-past five o'clock till about a quarter-past ten o'clock at night, when the prisoner and another man came—it was a very moonlight night—I had an opportunity of seeing the men—I am certain the prisoner is one—I and the other offered laid down, and the two men took each a sack on their backs—we got up, they ran away, and threw down their sacks of apples—we heard them fall—they made their escape—I took the basket, and left the other officer in charge of the apples—I went along the Woolwich-road towards the turnpike, and coming back I met the other man who had been with the prisoner, coming out of a cottage where the prisoner lived—in consequence of what he said I went towards the White Horse, Charlton—I followed him, and just as we got between there and Lord Howick's I saw the prisoner and the other man coming along the foot-path together—that man went to meet the prisoner, and they returned together—five or six yards before they came to me the prisoner, left him, and went to the other side of the road—I bid them good night, and walked on—I then returned to the turnpike-gate and met a young man who went with me into the wood—we were going to where I had left Smith—I heard two men talking in the garden of a cottage adjoining the wood—Smith held up his cutlass, and said, "Be quite"—the tall man that made his escape, came up a ladder, and put a dog
into the wood—and followed him—Smith laid hold of him, and took him at that time—I saw the prisoner at the foot of the ladder, and pursued him down the walk of the garden—he ran—I came back and put the handcuffs on the other man—the prisoner left his hat, which I took up—he afterwards owned it—I after that went into the cottage, and saw the prisoner—I told him I should take him—he seemed rather confused, and said, "What apples?"—I said it was no use making any resistance, they should both go together—I looked about, and under the bed I picked up these shoes, they were wet and muddy—I asked whose they were?—he said they were his—he then said, "Stop, let me see"—he said, "No, I do not think they are"—I threw them under the bed among four or five old pairs—, "Now, said I look out your shoes," and he looked out the same pair again—when I went down to the cottage Smith brought the other man down the foot-path—I found the cottage fastened—the other man said "It is no use, open the door, Jack —we then went in—the next morning I went to the wood again, and found these other two sacks of apples—the sacks I found first were not Mr. Farquhar's, but those I found on the Monday morning are his—I found them close by the gap that leads from the garden to the wood, with some sheeps' feet and part of a skin and head—the walnuts were in the sack where the apples were—I afterwards compared these shoes with the marks I had previously covered over—they went into the footmarks as completely as possible—they could not fit better—I am certain they were made by them—the gardener also tried them—I conveyed the prisoner to Newgate last Tuesday—he said it was a bad job, but they could not give him more than fourteen years.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say a word of this conversation before the Magistrates? A. No—I could not, he was committed at the time—I went into the wood from half-past four to five o'clock, and stayed there till a quarter past ten o'clock—that was the time the men first came into the wood—it had gone ten o'clock—I am certain that is the right hour—I got to the cottage after the man ran away—I met the prisoner in the road before I saw him in the cottage—when I met him it was, I suppose, very near 11 o'clock: because when I came back, Mr. Watson, of the White Horse, was about to put his shutters up; that was above half an hour after I saw him in the wood—it was near 11 o'clock when I met him in the road—I wished the tall one good night, because the prisoner crossed the road—I passed them both—I was in plain clothes—the tall one had seen me, I know, because I spoke to him when he came out of the cottage—I do not say that he knew me to be a policeman—I put the shoe alongside of the mark, and it was the same length—many working people's shoes are made very much alike, but some are longer than others—there was no occasion for any pressure—when I asked him about the shoes, he said they were not his, then he hesitated, and then picked them out again—I saw him at the foot of the ladder—he had shoes on then.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Was there time between the period when the men first came to the sacks, and the time you afterwards saw them on the road to go the cottage and change their shoes? A. Yes—it could not take them five minutes to go the wood—I gave them an answer to make them think I was going to Woolwich—they were not more than four minutes under my view when they came for the apples—when I met the prisoner again I saw him for five minutes—I knew him directly; and when I
saw him the third time, I followed him directly, and saw him in the cottage—I put the shoe into the marks gently, and watched the nails and tips go into the impression—there were three or four very plain marks.
HAMMOND SMITH ( police-constable R 75.) I was with Wild when the men came for the apples—I noticed the countenances of the men—the prisoner is one I am positive—I afterwards took a man at the top of a ladder and saw the prisoner underneath—I am positive he is the same person.
Cross-examined. I believe you had the misfortune to let your prisoner go? Yes—I had them both at the time the tall one made his escape—the other policeman was gone to the corner of the wood—I called to him, but the other got back to the house and got away.
HENRY CLARK . I am coachman to Mr. Farquhar. I believe I have seen this basket—I would not be positive to it.
JOSEPH HILL GREGORY re-examined. Q. There were some walnuts lost? A. Yes—there are some at the bottom of the sack among the apples—this is the gathering basket which I lost—I know it by three little marks on the that I made with my knife.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you make these marks since the apprehension of the prisoner? A. No—they have been there ever since I have, about eleven months.
MR. DOANE. called
JAMES WATSON . I am the landlord of the White Horse, within half a mile of this place. On Sunday night the 27th of last month, I recollect seeing the prisoner at my house—he was there about three o'clock in the afternoon, and staid there till ten minutes before eleven o'clock—that was the time he went—I went into the tap-room between eight and nine o'clock, he was then asleep—when I went in at ten o'clock he was in company with one or two more, drinking some beer—at half-past ten o'clock I took him in a pot of beer—he said, "I shall not pay you for it, I did not get my money on Saturday night—I shall receive my money from Mr. Ledger, at Deptford"—at ten minutes to eleven o'clock he came to the bar to my wife, and asked what beer he had had—he said he would pay to-morrow—I went into my tap-room, and it was ten minutes to eleven o'clock—I shut up at a quarter before eleven o'clock—I am quite positive that between a quarter past ten o'clock and ten minutes before eleven o'clock the prisoner was on my premises.
MR. BODKIN. Q. How far is your house from the wood? A. About half a mile—within about fifty yards—the turnpike is a quarter of a mile, and the wood is about three hundred yards farther—it would take seven or eight minutes to walk there, if he walked fast—I have not heard that Mr. Farquhar walked it in four minutes—I served the prisoner with a pint of porter at three o'clock, just before I sat down to dinner—I cannot go in without seeing the clock—I saw him next at five o'clock when I took the candles into the tap-room—he was sitting in company with or three others—I did not look at the clock then—I judged the time by the darkness—I saw him next between eight and nine o'clock—I judged the time because I had been taking out some beer, and when I went in it might be twenty minutes past eight o'clock—I saw him two or three times after, while I went backwards and forwards to the room—I saw him at ten o'clock—I looked at the clock then for the purpose of ascertaining the time—it might be a minute before or after—it was as near ten o'clock as possible there were three or four persons there, one of the
name of Guyer, one Lock, and some soldiers—the prisoner was drinking with them—my son attends customers—he was gone out, and the two younger ones were gone to bed—my servant left me about a week before—the prisoner had on a white flap cap—I did not see a tall stout man there—I know the prisoner's brother—I cannot say whether he was there—I was attending the two rooms, and was out with my beer from eight o'clock till a quarter past—the prisoner went out alone—I saw no person with him—his brother is employed by a person opposite me, and was paid at my house—his name is William Taylor—I did not see him at all that evening—I did tell the policeman that I believed it was the prisoner's brother that did the job, from the description he gave of the man.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.

204. MARY WILKINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of November, 1 cloak, value 13 s.; and 1 shawl, value 2 s.; the goods of Mary Turner.
MARY TURNER . I live at the house of Joseph Brown, in Black Horsefields, Deptford; he is a dealer in wood. On the 21st November, as returning home, I met the prisoner in Flagon-row, and asked what she was doing—she said she had no where to go—I said she might come and sleep on my chairs till morning—I went up stairs for about two minutes—when I came down she was gone, and I missed my things.
Prisoner. She was intoxicated, and came and asked me to see he home—she lent me these things. Witness. I did not.
CHARLES REEVE . I am apprentice to William Arthur, Church-street, Greenwich. On the 22nd of November, about six o'clock, the prisoner pledged a shawl in the name of "Mary King, Shepherd's-court."
BENJAMIN LOVELL . ( police-sergeant R 15.) I found the prisoner in Hughes's-fields, Deptford, about one o'clock in the morning of the 1st of December—I taxed her with having taken the cloak and shawl—she said she did not go home with any woman—I found the cloak on the bad she was lying on—her mother said that she brought it home on Monday night—the prisoner afterwards told me she took it from distress—I I have taken her home many times, but she would not stay at home—the prosecutrix was quite sober.
MARY TURNER re-examined. I did not lend her the things—she never asked me for them—she told me her father and mother had turned her out of doors.
NOT GUILTY .
