206. CHARLES MAHONEY was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of October, 1 pair of braces, value 1 s.; 1 shirt, value 2 s.; 1 pair of trowsers value 5 s.; 1 waistcoat, value 5 s.; 3 handkerchiefs value 3 s.; and 1 pair of stockings value 6 d.; the goods of Daniel Desmond.
ELLEN DESMOND . I am the wife of Daniel Desmond, and live in Collyer's-rents, Borough. The prisoner came to lodge at our house on Thursday evening the 13th of October—a woman, a neighbour, brought him—I was very ill in bed, having laid in only eight days, and I had a child lying dead—I agreed to let him the lodging at 2 s. a week—he said he might stop only a night or two, or a month—he slept there that night—we had a wake—I was in bed, and do not know what time he went to bed—he want away about seven o'clock in the morning, and these clothes were gone—I had several other lodgers—the prisoner was taken six weeks afterwards—he went away without paying for his lodging, and did not say he was going.
DANIEL DESMOND . I know the clothes were all missed five minutes after the prisoner was gone—I had seen them the night before—they were all in my charge, and the lodgers look to me to pay for them.
JOHN KEMP ( police-constable M. 60.) I apprehended the prisoner last Wednesday in Bird-cage-alley, and found a pair my braces, on him.
DANIEL DESMOND re-examined. These are my braces, which I lost when he went away—the things were worth 2 l.
GUILTY of stealing the braces. — Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18361128-207]
207. HENRY CHILDS was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of November, 2 shovels, value 4 s., and 1 brace, value 1 s., the goods of Robert Griffiths.
JAMES GRIFFITHS . I keep the Queen Adelaide public-house, at Wandsworth On Thursday, the 17th of November, the prisoner came to me, and said a barge of coals had come as far as the distillery, and he could not get it farther at that time—that he worked for the distillery and had some tools to dispose of—I said I was no judge of them—he went and brought Knight, who I employ, and he asked 5 s. for them—Knight said they were worth the money, and I gave him 5 s. for them,—I observed the initials "R G" on the shovels, and found they did not correspond with the prisoner's name—I then applied to the police, and he was taken up.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What is your business? A. I do the lightering for a brewhouse—I did not ask the prisoner his name, nor where he lived—Knight knew him.
JOHN BUSAIN . I am a police-inspector at Wandsworth. Griffiths applied to me on Saturday, respecting this, and left the tools in my possession to find the owner—he described the prisoner, and I had him apprehended.
WILLIAM REED ( police-constable V. 95.) I apprehended the prisoner at the Ship at Wandsworth—he said at the station-house that he had bought the tools.
ROBERT GRIFFITHS . I am a coal-merchant, and live at Belmont-place, Wandsworth-road. These tools are mine, and have my mark on them—I lost them from a wharf, three or four miles from the public-house they were sold at.
Cross-examined. Q. What did they cost you? A. About 20 s —I have known the prisoner some years as a lighterman—I believe I had seen the things the previous day.
WILLIAM LAMBERT . I am a coal-porter. I used these tools on Wednesday, the 16th of November on Mr. Griffiths's wharf—I saw nothing of the prisoner.
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Justice Vaughan.
[trialAccount t18361128-208]
208. CHARLES LEWIS was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying John Lott.
WILLIAM DAVIS . I am a seaman belonging to the Glamorgan of Cardiff. The deceased, John Lott, was mate of the Emerald Isle —on the night of last Sunday week I was on board the brig, near Pickle-herring tier—there was a vessel between the Glamorgan and the brig—they were in the same tier—Lott, myself, and a witness, were going on board our own vessel—we had to cross the brig the prisoner was in to get to our vessel—I went on board my own vessel, and stood on the deck—I heard a dog bark, and Lott said if the dog would bite him he would kill it—it was on board the brig—I do not know who it belonged to—the prisoner said it he struck the dog he would strike him—they then began to curse each other, and the prisoner took off his jacket—I did not hear him say any thing then—I saw his arm up, he struck at Lott—I cannot say whether the blow reached him, but the man fell overboard and was drowned—I had not seen any blows exchanged between them—Lott was a within about two feet and a half of the the gunwale—I ran to assist, to try to save him, but he fell between the two vessels—he did not appear very drunk—he walked steadily enough, and seemed collected and sensible—he appeared to have been been drinking—I think it must have been from the blow the he fell overboard—there was no gangway up.
Q. Did you observe whether he stepped back to avoid a blow, and might have fallen over before he was struck, or not? A. I could not observe that—Lott's arms were down when Lewis's were up—I was sober myself—I saw the prisoner next morning at Union Hall—he said it was a bad job, and he was very sorry for it, and he cried—he owned before the policeman that he was tipsy.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you say before the Coroner that you did not know whether any blow had reached him? A. Yes—It was a moonlight night—the bulwark was unshipped, and there was nothing to save him from falling—he stood sideways to the vessel, the prisoner was ahead—the deceased stood abaft of him—I am quite sure of that.
COURT. Q. Supposing the blow to have reached him, would he have fallen in the direction he did from the blow? A. Yes, as there was nothing to prevent him—they were facing one another, and his side rather to the gunwale.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Which way was the prisoner's left hand? A. It was up—if he struck the deceased with his left hand he would have struck him towards the gunwale—he stood on the larboard side of the vessel—I never saw the deceased strike him—I was not asked before the Coroner which hand the prisoner had up—I did not observe whether the deceased struck the dog or not—the prisoner went with others in a boat to try to save the man—I did not go into the boat myself—I said so before the Coroner—we had all been drinking—I think it was his left hand the prisoner struck with, because I do not think the man would have fallen overboard if he had struck with his right hand—he would have knocked him in another direction.
LEWELLYN DAVIES . I am a seaman on board the Emerald Isle. On the night in question I was on board the Glamorgan —the prisoner and deceased were on the deck of the brig—I saw Lewis pull off his jacket, lift his arm up, and strike Lott—Lott had not raised his arm to strike
him—Lott fell overboard—he was standing about two feet from the gunwale—it only a single blow.
Cross-examined. Q. Which hand did he strike with? A. His right hand, I am sure of that—I cannot say whether the blow reached Lett—if it had, it would have struck him from the water—the blow was made at his head or shoulder—they were standing near each other—Lott was abaft —I did not notice whether he staggered before he fell—his face was turned towards the head of the ship—I was about half tipsy—the party were all fresh.
Q. Is it not very easy, when a bulwark is unshipped, for the mere motion of a man's body to cause him to slip off vessel? A. Yes—this was a frosty night—the decks were frosty and slippery—the vessel was lying quite still and quiet—there was no wind—I cannot undertake to say whether the man fell in consequence of a blow or push or not—I went to look for the body, and two or three more came after me in a boat—I cannot say who they were—I saw the body after it was found—it was the body of John Lott.
JOHN MACHIN . I am Captain, of the brig Barnsley. I was in bed—I heard an alarm, and came on deck—I saw the deceased swimming in the water, and made an attempt top save him—he had got hold of our cable, and he pulled himself partly out of the water—I go into a boat, but he was lost—the prisoner was in our boat trying to pick him up.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he appear very eager to save the deceased? A. Yes—the decks were very slippery—the ship was down about eighteen inches, slant with her head—she was afloat, but he cargo laid forwards—the prisoner was very much affected at this—I have known him from a boy—I never saw him out of temper with any one—I never saw the deceased swim—the tide was very strong, and drew him under the vessel's bottom.
JOHN WEST . I am a fisherman. I found the body two tiers beyond where he was lost, and took him ashore.
(The prisoner received an excellent character.)
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined One Week.
Before Lord Chief Justice Denman
[trialAccount t18361128-209]
209. LUKE GRADY was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of November 1 ton weight of coals, value 16 s., the goods of the Vauxhall Waterworks Company, his masters: and JOHN CURLEY , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Proseuction.
GEORGE RYDER PEPPERCORN . I am clerk to the Vauxhall Water-works Company, Grady was in their employ as carman on the 22nd of November—Curley had been occasionally, as an extra man—it was part of Grady's employment to move coals from the store of the Company in Cumberland Gardens to Kennington-lane.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long has Grady been in the Company's employ? A. About three years—they employ about sixteen labourers.
JOHN STEWART . I am employed on the Company's premises as engineer. On Tuesday the 22nd of November Grady loaded some coals at the stores at seven o'clock in the morning—he drove that load away, and returned about twenty minutes before eight o'clock and loaded another load—I
assisted him in doing it, and saw him leave the yard—I do not know what he did with the load.
FREDERICK SKITTERELL . I am a policeman. I was on duty in Vauxhall-street on the 22nd of November, and saw Grady drive the cart from Vauxhall-street into Prince's-road, and shoot the coals out of the cart opposite No.58—the prisoner Curley then came out of that house with a clothes-basket and shovel, filled the basket with coals, and carried it into the house—he then returned for a second basket—as he was filling that, I said to him that he had got a dirty job—he said he had—I went to the Water-works in Kennington-lane, and made inquiry—I then went to Cumberland Gardens, and made further inquiry—I then returned to Kennington-lane, and saw Kennelly, and by that time Grady came up the yard without either horse or cart—Kennelley inquired of him how many loads of coals he had brought from Cumberland Gardens—he said only one, and that he had just delivered—I asked him where he brought the load from which, he delivered in Prince's road—he said he had not taken one there—I detained him, and he was given in charge—I am sure he is the man—I have known him four years—it was about a quarter past seven o'clock in the morning.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you say things before the Magistrate about speaking to Grady about the coals? A. Yes, and also about his saying he had but one load of coals—it was taken down, and read over to me.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Did the man who drove the cart go away directly he shot the coals? A. Yes—Curley came our before the cart was out of sight.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Must any body acquainted with the cart know what sort of a one it was? A. Yes.
EDWARD KENNELLEY . I am employed at the Gas-works at Kennington. On the 23rd of November Grady brought one load of coals the first thing in the morning—he did not bring two, to my knowledge—I afterwards asked him, in the policeman's presence, whether he had brought one load or two—he told me one load—I had not been at the office all the morning—I came there at a little before or after six o'clock—when the policeman gave me information it was about half-past seven o'clock, up to that time there had not been two loads, delivered to my knowledge—there might have been without my knowing—I heard nothing said to Grady about another load.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Then you do not know of your own knowledge that only one load was delivered? A. No—I saw one delivered—I was at the gate when it came in—it was all thrown down in the yard—I was in the office till the policeman came—there is not any window into the yard—it is possible another load might be delivered without my knowing it—I could not tell that from the quantity—there is a door-way to my office into the yard, and that was open.
RICHARD HOWARD ( police-constable L. 83.) On the morning of the 22nd of November, about half-past eight o'clock, I went down Prince's-road, from information I had received from Skitterell—When I got to No. 58. I found the pavement washed quite clean—it seemed as if coals had soiled the road—directly I got there Curley came out of No. 58 with a sack with some coals on his shoulder—asked him what he had got there?—he said, "Coals"—I said, "What are you going to do with them?"—he said, "To take them to my sister's in place of some I borrowed"—I took him into custody.
(William Ramage, builder, Gloucester-street, Queen-square; and William Davis, Green-street, Leicester-square, gave Grady a good character)
GRADY— GUILTY . Aged 33.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
CURLEY— GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined One Year.
Before Lord Chief Justice Denman.
[trialAccount t18361128-210]
210. GEORGE BARNES, JAMES BROWN , and JAMES WILLIAMS were indicted for felonioulsy breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Charlotte Luton, on the 17th of October, at St. Mary, Lambeth, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 pair of decanters, value 1 l., 1 set of fire-irons, value 5 s.; 1 hearth-rug, value 1 l.; 1 table-cover, value 10 s.; 5 spoons, value 1 l.; 1 hearth-rug, value 1 l.; 1 table-cover, umbrella, value 6 s.; 3 shawls, value 3 l.; 1 pair of ear-rings, value 1 l.; 4 rings, value 3 l.; 1 opera-glass, value 5 s.; and one card-case, value 2 s.; 6 d.; the goods of the said Charlotte Luton: and MATILDA HARDIN for felonioulsy receiving part of the same goods, well knowing, them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
CHARLOTTE LUTON . I am a widow, and live in Basing-place, Waterloo-road, in the parish of Lambeth. On Monday night, the 16th of October, I was the last person up—I went to bed at eleven o'clock—I fastened the house up entirely—I was alarmed about half-past six o'clock in the morning by an opposite neighbour knocking at my street door—I came down stairs, and missed the articles stated, from the parlours—I had seen them all there the evening before—I found the street door wide open, and also the staircase window—the persons must have entered by the staircase window, by getting over the side wall from the street—it is a sash-window—I cannot say whether it was fastened over-night, but it was shut—there was no appearance of violence on it—if it had been left unfastended a person outside could have thrown it up—I have seen all the articles, and have no doubt they are my property.
JANE MANN . My husband is an egg-merchant, and lives in Agnes-street, Waterloo-road, On Monday morning, the 17th of October, at a quarter before six o'clock, I hear footsteps under my window—I live opposite Mrs. Luton—the street is about twenty yards wide—I threw my window open, and saw the prisoner Barnes—it was quite daylight—I had never seen him before—he removed from the window to the end of the street looking into Waterloo-road, and a few minutes after I saw Brown open Mrs. Luton's street door, and whistle—Barnes then crossed the road, and Brown came out of the house with two bundles, one was in a red cloth,—they looked up at the window and saw me, and then Barnes ran up the street—Mrs. Luton's staircase window was open, but the blind was down—Brown returned into the house—I went down and awoke Mr. Hancock, My landlord, suspecting it was a robbery—I then returned to my window again, and staid there till exactly half-past six o'clock, when I saw Brown and Williams come out of the house—I feel certain of Williams as well—Brown had a small bundle, and Williams a hearth-rug and fire-irons—they left the door wide, open, and I asked Mr. Hancock to knock at the door, which he did—Mrs. Luton came down, and in half an hour she fetched me over to her house, and complained of missing several things—I did not know her before—I said before the Magistrate I felt certain that these two were the prisoners, but could not swear to them—I think Williams to be the man I saw with the fire-irons.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You only saw him a very short time? A. No, only crossing the road—it was seven weeks ago—it is a very quiet street.
JOHN HANCOCK . Mrs. Mann called me up on the morning in question, and I went out and knocked at the door, and I saw two youths come out—I could not positively swear to them—one was considerably shorter than the other—the tallest had a small bundle under his arm, and the short one a set of fire-irons and a hearth-rug—the prisoners' dress and height correspond with the boys who came of the house, but I had no opportunity of seeing their features.
PRICE JONES BULLOCK . I am shopman to Mr. Jones, a pawnbroker in Tothill-street. On Monday morning, the 17th of October, I took in a set of fire-irons, decanters, and shawls from the prisoner Hardin, between eleven and twelve o'clock, in the name of "Ann Jones, No.14, Strutton-ground."
MARY ATTREE . I am the wife of Charles Attree, a shoemaker. The prisoner Hardin lodged in my house—she came in January last, and remained till the officers came after her, when she ran away—I have seen Brown and Williams repeatedly at my house with her—I told her I wished her not to allow them to come—she said they were her nephews, and where she was they should come.
Barnes's Defence. On Friday, the 17th of October, I was at work with my father, from half-past nine o'clock in the morning till nine o'clock at night, at Billingsgate-market.
(The prisoner, Hardin, put in a written Defence, stating that she had taken Brown to lodge with her, and positively denying having pawned the property, and that before the Magistrate the pawnbroker had not identified her.)
PRICE JONES BULLOCK re-examined. I did express a little doubt about Hardin at Queen-square—I am sure she is the person—I have very little doubt indeed—I cannot positively swear to her.
ANN JANE TEASDALL . I am a nurse. I know that the prisoner Hardin was ill, and not out of her house from the 14th to the 21st of October—she was not confined to her bed, but to the house.
BARNES— GUILTY .
BROWN, WILLIAMS, and HARDIN— NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Vaughan.

211. GEORGE BARNES, JAMES BROWN , and JAMES WILLIAMS were again indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Charles Henry Braggini, on the 18th of October, and stealing therein, 13 spoons, value 2 l.; 1 coat, value 1 l.; 1 pair of spectacles, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 pocket-book value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Julian Braggini, since deceased.
CHARLES HENRY BRAGGINI . I live in Hoxton-square, in the parish of St. Leonard, Shoreditch. I lost some property—I do not know on what day—my father, Julian Braggini, lived with me at the time—I understand some of his property was taken also—I do not know how the house was entered—I was out at the time—none of the property is here.
RICHARD EDWARDS . I am chief-clerk at Queen-square Office. The late Julian Braggini gave some evidence, during his life time, which I took down in the prisoners' presence—I heard him sworn, and it was read over to him in the prisoners presence—( read)—"Julian Braggini, says, I live in my son's Charles Henry Braggini's, house, and am a merchant—on Friday afternoon last, about half-past one o'clock Lowe came and spoke
to me—I went into my parlour and saw a drawer open, where we kept the silver spoons, and found ten silver tea-spoons, two salt-spoons, and a mustard-spoon, missing; I also missed black coat off the sofa in the on the sofa half an hour before—I found the window sash thrown up, and the blinds open—the fastening of the window sash was broken before—when I put my coat on the sofa, the window was down, and the blinds too.
Signed, JULIAN BRAGGINI."
EDWARD LANGLEY . I am a policeman. On Friday, the 28th of October, I saw the three prisoners together's going up King-street, Westminster, towards Charing-cross, about 12 o'clock—I saw them afterwards coming through Smithfield towards Westminster in a cab, a little before four—they got out of the cab and went into James's-court, Westminster—I followed them into the house, and went to room, the door of which was fastened within—I knocked, and it was opened—I found the three prisoners and two females in the room—all the prisoners had got their coats and hats off—I searched them, and found on Barnes 12 s.—1 s. on Brown, and another constable who was with me found 9 s. 6 d. on Williams—we did not take them into custody at the time—I took Williams next day, and found a bunch of keys on him—two of them have the wards filed out to make them skeleton keys.
JOHN LOWE . I am a watch-finisher, and live with my father, who is clerk at a wharf, and lives at Hoxton. On Friday, the 28th of October, about a quarter past one o'clock in the day I was in Hoxton-square, and saw three boys in the corner of the square together, and they parted—I know Brown and Williams to be two of them—I saw Williams stand in the gate of the prosecutor's house, and Brown up in the corner—I saw Barnes go in at the window—Brown at that time was up in the corner, and Williams against the gate—I did not see the window lifted up—I saw him get in at the window, but did not see him lift the sash—I then went to the National School to see my brother, and as I came back I saw Williams and Barnes coming towards Brown with a coat—they went away together, and I went and told the prosecutor—I am sure I saw them all together—I believe I did not tell the Magistrate I saw him lift the sash—I might say so in my flurry, never bring at such a place before.
Barnes's Defence. On Friday, the 28th of October, I went with Williams and Brown to his father's house, in Brick-lane—he was about the house a quarter of an hour, and I and Williams stopped at the public-house—he came out of his father's and took a cab, being tired, and went home.
Brown's Defence. I went to my father's to get a clean shirt—it was not ready—I wanted to see a person at Somers'-town—I went there and returned—when my shirt was ready I put it on, and came to them at the public-house, and took a cab and went with them.
Williams's Defence. I was with Barnes.
BARNES— GUILTY . Aged 19.
BROWN— GUILTY . Aged 19.
WILLIAMS— GUILTY . Aged 19.
Transported for Life.
(There was a third indictment against the prisoners.)
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

JOHN MORTON . I am a medical labeller, and live in Blackfriars-road. On the 1st of December, I lost a large glass jar, worth 2 l —I met the prisoner with it in his hands, three doors from my shop, going towards Friar-street.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you see him in the shop? A. No—he said somebody had given it him to carry—I have no private mark on it, but the ornamental work I did myself—I had seen it safe the day—before my shop front had been beat in with the heavy gale.

214. HUMPHERY COURTNEY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of November, 92lbs. weight of lead, value 11 s., the goods of George Baker, his master.
THOMAS FIELDER . I am foreman to George Baker, of New-row, Lambeth—Mr. Samuel Baker has been dead about three weeks—he was a builder. On the 19th of November, we had some lead at Palace New-row, and at a variety of places—I cannot say where this was taken from—the lead is similar to some that was in our store-house a month since—that would be the 28th or 29th of October, and at that time Mr. Samuel Baker was alive, and he and his son then had the business—it was Samuel Baker and Son—the prisoner worked on the premises—he was working there on the 19th—we had lead similar to this, and it was missing—he has had a very good character.
HENRY SMITH ( police-constable S 44.) On the 19th of November, at half-past seven o'clock, I was on duty in York-road, Lambeth—I saw the prisoner carrying this basket, and the lead in it—I asked what he had got—he said "Lead;" that he brought it from home, and that a boy sold it him for 6 d.—from the quantity I thought it was not likely he would get it for 6 d., and took into custody.
THOMAS FIELDER re-examined. I cannot swear to this—the piece we lost was similar—the prisoner would have no opportunity of going into our place at half-past seven o'clock in the evening—he left at twenty minutes before six o'clock—York-road is about five hundred yards from our place.
Prisoner's Defence. One of my men called me away at a quarter-past five o'clock, and took me into a public-house, and he said a friend of his had a piece of lead there that he brought from a rail-road—he asked me to take
it to Blackfriars-road, and I took it—I was so struck I did not know what I said to Smith.
NOT GUILTY .

215. THOMAS DORRINGTON was indicted for embezzlement.

JAMES REID . I received a basket of plants from the prisoner on the 9th, and paid him 1 l. 10 s. 6 d. for his master.

216. BENJAMIN MORRIS was indicated for stealing, on the 2nd of November, 1 pail value 1 s.; 12lbs. weight of fat, value 4 s.; 1 gut, value 6 d.; and 3 gallons of blood, value 6 d.; the goods of Frederick Brown, his master.
FREDERICK BROWN . I am a butcher, and live in Lambeth-walk. I employed the prisoner occasionally to kill for me—on the evening of the 1st of November he came to work, and on the 2nd of again—on Monday. I missed 12lbs. weight of fat and a pail—he was standing at a green-shop opposite—I called him over, and said, "Where is the rest of this fat?"—he said, "I do not know, indeed, master"—I said, "Do you go and fetch it, and then I will not say any more about it"—he said, "I know nothing about it"—I pulled the other fat open, and said, "You see it is not all here"—he said, "No, it is not"—I said, "Go and fetch it"—he said he knew nothing about it, and gave him in charge—we went to his house, and there I found my pail, with the fat in his cupboard—he said he did not know how it came there.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When you went to this house was the policeman with you? A. Yes—the prisoner might have had the pail, he had it once, I believe, to fetch some grains in, he then asked for it—I have a person in my employ named Foulkes—the prisoner never said in my presence that he had the pail lent to him for a temporary purpose—he never bought skins and offal of me.
COURT. Q. Have you any doubt that the fat belonged to you? A. Not at all.
JOHN JEFFERIS . I am a pork-butcher. The prisoner called on me on the 2nd of November—I asked if he was going to kill any thing—he said he was—I asked him to let me have a set of guts and a pail of blood, and about eight o'clock he sent the blood down, and brought the gut himself, but I was not present—I went to Mr. Brown—he came and saw this blood and gut.
FREDERICK BROWN . I missed the gut, and went there and found it.
MR. DOANE called
JOSEPH LAWFORD . I live at No. 16, Mansion House-street, Bermondsey. I saw the prosecutor foreman—he told me that he had lent Morris the pail himself, and that Mr. Brown knew it.
FREDERICK BROWN . It is an entire falsehood—I went to Jefferis house, and saw some blood and some gut—the gut was missing from my slaughter-house.
GUILTY . Aged 39.— Confined Six Months; Three Weeks Solitary.

217. JAMES DONOVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, 1 pie-can, value 12 s., 12 patty pans, value 1 s. 6 d.; 36 pies, value 2 s.; and one apron, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Edward Jackson his master.
EDWARD JACKSON . On the 13th of April the prisoner came and asked me to let him have some goods to take out to sell, as his father and he had had some little differences, and he was afraid to go home—I let him, have 3 s. worth of pies, allowing him sixpence in the shilling for selling them—I never saw him again till the 15th of month, when he was in custody, he said that he had been in the Queen of Spain's service, and just come home—the pies were my property—I let him have them on credit—he was to bring me the money every night—I lent him the can and pans.
Prisoner. He trusted me with the pies, and lent me a stool, and a white apron; and in going over London—bridge, the wind blew some fire into a gentleman's face, and he turned and broke the leg of the stool.
GUILTY. Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Months.

219. MARY COLLINS was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of November, 5 spoons, value 10 s.; and 2 rings, value 8 s.; the goods of Benjamin Bullivant Warren.
BENJAMIN BULLIVANT WARREN . I live in Bunhill-place, Southwark. The prisoner had been in our employ for six or seven weeks—on the 18th of November my mother sent her up stairs to make a bed—and when she went to church on Sunday week I went to a private drawer in that room and missed the spoons and rings—I left the drawer as I found it—she came again on the Tuesday, and went up stairs again to make the bed—I was under the room, and heard the drawer drawn out—I went up about three o'clock—as soon as I could, and missed the tongs—these are the spoons and rings—this is my wife's, wedding-ring.
WILLIAM TAYLOR ( police-constable M 79.) I searched the prisoner, but found nothing on her—I told her she would be searched at the station-house by a female—she then desired Mrs. Warren to go backwards, with her, and then she gave her the sugar-tongs—I then took her home, and she took a bundle out of the cupboard, and gave me eleven duplicates, four of which she said were her own.
JOSEPH EDWIN CRESSWELL . I am a pawnbroker. I produce two of the ten-spoons—I have a corresponding duplicate with the one before me—they were pledged by a woman in the name of Collins—I do not know who.
HENRY COTTAM . I am a pawnbroker, and live in the Borough. I
have one spoon which was pledged by a female, in the name of "Collins"—I have the corresponding ticket to the one produced by the officer.
JOSEPH CLOTHIER . I am a pawnbroker. I produce a ring which was pledged by a female in the name of "Gardiner"—I do not think it was the prisoner.
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Confined Six Months; Four Weeks Solitary.

220. CATHERINE GREEN was indicated for stealing, on the 27th of November, 1 sovereign, and 1 shilling, the monies of Samuel Shepherd, from his person.
SAMUEL SHEPHERD . On Sunday last I went to the Rose and Crown, about a quarter before eleven o'clock at night—I only had one glass of run and one drink of beer—I had just come from my home, at North-place, Wandsworth—I paid 2 d. for the rum—I took it out of my pocket—I had then one sovereign, two shillings, and 2 d.—I spent no more there—I met the prisoner there, and she asked if I would go with her—I said I would—she lived on the Plain—I went to bed, but did not strip—I had the money then in my pocket, because I put the sovereign and one shilling into a bag by itself; the other shilling was in my pocket—it was meant for her—I did not tell her that was all she was to have—I missed the money when I got up from the bed—I did not stay there more than a quarter of an hour—I told her I missed my money, and she ran away—I did not give her the shilling—I followed her to the Rose and Crown again, quite close—she there spoke to a man who she lives with—I am sure of that—she was by the side of him—he is not here—the money has never been found.
Prisoner. The Chief of what he says is false—he did not accuse me of stealing any thing in the room—I asked him for 1 s., and he said he would not give me one. Witness. You did not ask me for any thing; you went off directly.
JOSEPH HALL ( police-constable V 37.) I took the prisoner, and found on her one shilling, one sixpence, and the key of the room—I searched the man half an hour afterwards, and found nothing on him but a few halfpence.
Prisoner. I was not sitting with the man I live with—I was on the opposite side of the room.
SAMUEL SHEPHERD , re-examined. I know the man well—he was in the Rose and Crown when we went away, and when we came back—I did not hear her speak to him—she sat on the bench by the side of him—I did not see her speak to any man going along, it was in the Rose and Crown.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18361128-221]
221. MARY OWEN MACK was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of October, 1 counterpane, value 4 s.; 1 sheet, value 1 s.; 1 gown, value 3 d.; and 1 apron, value 2 d.; the goods of Cornelius Ragan.
JULIA RAGAN . I am the wife of Cornelius Ragan; we keep a lodging-house in Flacon-court, Borough. On the 29th of October the prisoner came to my house and asked for a night's lodging; she was to pay 1 d.—she said she was in great distress; that she was serjeant's wife in the Queen of Spain's service, and on the Saturday following she was going to take 13 l. the next day, Friday, she staid all day with me—I found she was not going out—I gave her her dinner, and supper at night, and the
following morning she got up and went out—I missed these things—the gown was found on her back, and the apron—these are my property.
Cross-examined by MR. DUNBAR. Q. Is your husband living? A. Yes—I saw him this morning—I did not say that her gown was wet, and she could dry it—I did not lend her one to wear the next day—she lodged in the second floor—she took my property in the morning before I was out of bed.
JOHN WOODHOUSE . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner, and found the apron and gown on her, on the 4th of November—on her way to the station-house she told me she had left two duplicates at Mrs. Brown's✗ St. Giles's—I went there and found the two duplicates, one for a sheet and one for a gown, pledged at Mr. Basset's, Queen-street, Lincoln's Insfields—I went there and found them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you give her any caution? A. No—she had before told the prosecutor where the tickets were, and begged his to forgive her—he did not tell her it would be better for her in my hearing.
MARY BROWN . The prisoner came to my house, and left me two tickets on the 29th of October—I gave them to the officer.
BENJAMIN RICHARD MURRAY . I am a pawnbroker. The officer came to me, and I delivered the counterpane and sheet to him—I do not know who pawned them.
Cross-examined. Q. Does it ever happen that you put a wrong name on the property? A. No—if two persons pledge at the same time it cannot happen that the ticket of one should get on the other, because we✗ them on at the time.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Two Months; One Week Solitary.
[trialAccount t18361128-221a]
221. JOHN JOHNSON was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. SHEA conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN GILES LAMB . On the 5th of November I was in the employ of Mr. Ralph Fenwick, who keeps a shop in Prince's-place, Southwark, for the sale of squibs—the prisoner came for some squibs, and threw down shillings—I said I thought it was bad—I had given him fourpence change—I called Harrison to look at it—he bit it, and said it was bad—I did not put it into the till—I had no till—he had a dozen squibs, and put eightpence for them.
GEORGE HARRISON . On the 5th of November I assisted Lamb in Mr. Fenwicks's shop, in Princes-street—the prisoner came in for a dozen squibs—he laid down a shilling—I bit it, and gave it to Lamb—I went to another shop in Melina-place in five minutes after the prisoner had left Prince's street shop—when I got there I stopped about ten minutes, and then the prisoner came for 2 d. worth of squibs—he put a shilling on the counter—Miss-Pomeroy took it up—I took it to see whether it was good or bad—I gave it to Miss. Pomeroy—she gave it to me, and said, "Go and get change"—I ran to the shop in Prince's-street, and both the shillings were delivered to the officer, who was there at the time—he came over with me, and took the prisoner.
Prisoner. Q. When I came in, did I not put the shilling on the counter, and you said it was good? A. I did not—I said it was bad—Mr. Lamb did not put it into the till—there is none—you did not buy half a dozen—you paid 8 d. for a dozen of squibs.
JANE POMEROY . I was serving in the shop in Melina-place-the prisoner came for half a dozen of squibs which would come to 2 d.—he
threw down a shilling and a farthing on the counter—I took up the shilling and bit it, and gave it to Harrison to get change.
THOMAS DUNN . I am a constable. On the 5th of November I was coming from the Borough, about six o'clock at night, and called at Mr. Fenwick's shop—Lamb had got a bad shilling—Harrison came in and gave Lamb another shilling—in consequence of what Lamb said I went to the shop in Melina-place, and saw the prisoner there—I told him he had been passing bad shillings—he said he had not, or how could he do it, and began a unbutton his coat—he had nothing about him but one farthing—Harrison gave me two shillings—I gave them and the prisoner to a policeman.
ROBERT CREASY ( police-constable L 172.) On the 5th of November Dunn gave the prisoner and these two shillings into my charge.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin. These are both counterfeit, but not from the same die.
Prisoner's Defence. If they were bad I did not know it, as I took them in change in coming over Westminster-bridge, where I bought a comb—I did not look at them.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18361128-223]
223. JOHN SMITH was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MARY CARELESS . My husband keeps a shop in Robert-street, North Brixton, and sells tripe. On the 12th of November the prisoner came there, and bought 1/2lb. of sausages—he gave me a shilling—I gave him 9 1/2 d.—I said, "I do not like this shilling, I do not think it is good"—he said, "I wish I had one more than I could carry"—he went out, and I looked at it again, and found it was bad—I put it into a tea-pot on a shelf, and kept it till I gave it to Mr. Slaughter—there was no other with it—on the 14th of November, about nine o'clock in the morning, the prisoner came again for a red herring, which I put on the counter—he tendered another shilling—I saw it was the same sort of coin as he had given me before—I said I could not give him change—he said he would get change—I then sent for a policeman—Slaughter came before the prisoner was out of sight—I gave him the shilling I first took, after I had marked it.
JOHN MARTIN . I live in Bromley-row, Clapham, and sell shoemaker's materials. The prisoner came on the 14th of November about a quarter after ten o'clock for a pennyworth of nails, and gave me a bad shilling, I gave him 11 d.—while I was serving, he amused me with a tale of his having some onions to sell—I said I wanted none that prevented me from noticing the shilling so much as I might—I put it into my pocket—I had there two sixpences, and another shilling, a new one, which I had taken three quarters of an hour before—the shilling the prisoner gave me was the coin of George III—I had it in my pocket about two days—I soon after saw Slaughter—I did not give him the shilling till ordered by the Magistrate.
GEORGE SLAUGHTER ( police-constable P 152.) In consequence of what Mrs. Careless said, I went after the prisoner—I first saw him in the Clapham-road—I saw him go to Mr. Martin's shop—I waited till the came out, and took him—Mr. Martin gave me this shilling—I searched the prisoner, and found two good sixpences and 10 d. in copper—I told the prisoner I had got something else against him, for passing a shilling to Mrs. Careless—he said he had not been that road that day that he came from Wandsworth—I took him to the station-house.
Prisoner. I told you I had been to Martin's; and when you got to the door you said I had not been in there—I said I had, and I would go in—and he pulled out the two shillings, and said he did not know which I gave him—you
weighed them, and found one bad, and then said it must be the bad one I gave him. Witness. He pulled out two shillings, and I said, "Which is the one gave you?"—he said, "This is the one"—an then he took and put a shilling in one scale and the other in the other, and found it was bad—I never saw the prisoner till I found him in the Clapham-road—I followed him most of the way from Mr. Careless's—I saw him enter the shop at the front—I did not know then that it was Mr. Martin's—I followed him from Brixton to Clapham—I had not lost sight of him above two minutes.
MR. FIELD. These are both counterfeit, and from the same die.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined One Year.
First Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18361128-224]
224. EDWARD CRISP was indicted for a misdemeanor.
THOMAS LIFELY . I am a horse-keeper, and live at Battersea, Surrey. I belong to a club, of which John Mayhew is a member—we have a fund among ourselves to give relief to the members in sickness—I act as treasurer to pay the money—on the 6th of November the prisoner came to me on the subject of a person of the name of John Mayhew, to whom there was a sum of 5 s. due considering him as a member of the club—when the prisoner came he said that John Mayhew was very bad, his wife could not leave him to come, and had asked him (the prisoner) to come—he said he came for John Mayhew's sick money—I paid him 5 s.
ELLEN MAYHEW . I live in the parish of Wandsworth, Surrey—my husband was sick—I gave no direction to the prisoner to go Lifely on the 6th of November—I had on a former occasion, on the 23rd of October, to get the money due to my husband—he got 6 s. on the 26th, and brought it me home—he told me he had put it on the chimney-piece—I was not at home at the time—he told me of it, and found we had got 6 s —I did not send him the second time.
THOMAS LIFELY re-examined. On the 26th of October I paid the prisoner 10 s., and 5 s. on the 6th of November.
ELLEN MAYHEW re-examined. The prisoner did not bring any money to me or my husband on the 6th of November, nor had I sent him for it.
Prisoner. The first time you sent me was on Sunday morning—Thomas Lifely said he must see his master, and I went on Monday—he did not pay—on Tuesday he gave me 10 s.—I brought you 6 s., and you asked me to go on the 6th of November. Witness. No, I did not.
JOSEPH SMITH . I saw Lifely pay the prisoner 10 s. on the 26th of October, in Wandsworth parish, on the Tooting road.
TERENCE CLARK ( police-constable V 107.) I apprehended the prisoner in Wandsworth.
Prisoner. The women asked me to go on the 6th of November in the own house—I do not know that there was any body there.
ELLEN MAYHEW . I undertake to swear that I never authorized him to get money except on the 26th of October.
JURY. Q. Was he in the habit of coming backwards and forwards to see you husband? A. Yes—from their being brought up together in the same place—I cannot tell whether he was there on the 4th, 5th, or 6th of November, but I did not give him authority to receive this.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not go with me afterwards to see what was allowed? A. Yes; I did not send you three times before I went with you—I
did not know when I was to have any more, and I went with you to the place where I was to get it, but I did not see Mr. Lifely.
JURY. Q. Did the prisoner take you there? A. Yes, all along the railroad—he did not take me to any house—he went down to the stable—I did not go there—that was about a fortnight after I had the 6 s.—he went to the stable, and said the man was not there—I was going to hear what was due, and to get it myself—he never gave me the 5 s —the sum allowed was 5 s. a week.
THOMAS LIFELY re-examined. Q. Did he apply to you after the 6th of November? A. Yes, once more—I never saw the woman—I let him have 5 s. after the 6th of November.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Six Months.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, DECEMBER 12, 1836.
